We are grateful to Tiago Debarba and Karen Hovhannisyan for valuable comments and insights.
We acknowledge support by the EU COST Action MP1209 “Thermodynamics in the quantum regime”
and from the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) through the START project Y879-N27, the project P 31339-N27, and the joint Czech-Austrian project MultiQUEST (I 3053-N27 and GF17-33780L).
YG acknowledges support by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) through the Zukunftskolleg ZK03.

Appendix: Mathematical Model for Measurement Procedures

In this Appendix, we give a detailed description of
unbiased
measurement procedures introduced in the main text. As we have argued, ideal measurements (unbiased, faithful, and non-invasive) are not generally implementable in finite time or with finite energy. In practice, real measurements may nonetheless approximate ideal measurements by investing energy; loosely speaking, the approximation becomes better, the more energy that is invested. To make this more precise, we will explicitly determine the fundamental energy cost of projective
1
1

1

Arbitrary quantum measurements represented by positive-operator-valued measures (POVMs) can be realized by projective measurements on a Hilbert space obtained by appending an auxiliary system of, at most
[
35
]
, the same dimension as the original system. We therefore concentrate on projective measurements.
measurements.

A.I
Framework

System.
We consider a quantum system
S
with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
of dimension
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})
initially in an arbitrary unknown quantum state represented by a density operator
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})
, i.e., a Hermitian operator with
\mathrm{tr}(\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})=1
in the space of linear operators
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})
over the system Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. We are then interested in describing (projective) measurements of the system w.r.t. a basis
\{\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\}_{i}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, which we take to be the eigenbasis of the system Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, i.e., we can write
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\hbar\sum_{i}\Omega_{i}\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, where
\hbar(\Omega_{j}-\Omega_{i})
is the energy gap between the
i
-th and
j
-th levels. For instance, an example that we will focus on later is that of the simplest quantum system — a qubit — with vanishing ground state energy and energy gap
E_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\hbar\Omega
. That is,
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and the system Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
has eigenstates
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
and
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
and spectral decomposition
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=E_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
.

Pointer.
Similarly, we consider a pointer system
P
with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
of dimension
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})
and Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. We then take the resource-theoretic point of view that the pointer is initially in a state
that is freely available, i.e., a thermal state
\tau_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}(\beta)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})
at ambient temperature
T=1/\beta
.
We order the spectrum of the pointer Hamiltonian in terms of its excitations into
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
sectors of size
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}/d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, i.e.,
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}/d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}\left|\right.\!{E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}}\!\left.\right|
with
E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}\leq E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k^{\prime})$}}}_{j}\ \forall i,j
for
k^{\prime}>k
.
The thermal (Gibbs) state is given by

\displaystyle\tau_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}/d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}p^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}\left|\right.\!{E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}}\!\left.\right|

(A.1)

where
p^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}=\exp\bigl(-\beta E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}\bigr)/\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}
is the pointer’s partition function
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{tr}(e^{-\beta H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}})=\sum_{i,k}e^{-\beta E^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(k)$}}}_{i}}
.

Measurement procedure.
We now wish to consider a measurement of the system’s energy, i.e., of the observable
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, or, in other words, a projective measurement of the system in the energy eigenbasis
2
2

2

Note that a projective measurement of the system in any other (orthonormal) basis can be subsumed into this discussion by including an additional unitary transformation (and its energy cost) on the initial state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
to switch between the energy eigenbasis and the desired measurement basis.
. The corresponding
measurement procedure
may be defined via a completely positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) map
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
, where
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, that maps
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\tau_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
to a post-measurement state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
. This may be understood as a generalized interaction between the system, the pointer, and some auxiliary system. Here, we do not wish to address the question of which measurement outcome is ultimately realized (which pure state the system is left in), or how and why this may be the case. That is, we do not attempt to make statements about what is often perceived as the “measurement problem”, but rather take the point of view that system and pointer are left in a joint state in which the internal states of the system are correlated with the internal states of the pointer. Each of the latter is designated to correspond to one of the system states
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, such that, upon finding the pointer in a state chosen to reflect
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, one concludes that the measurement outcome is “
i
”. More precisely, each eigenstate
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
of the measured observable of the system is assigned a set
\{\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\}_{n}
of orthogonal states of the pointer corresponding to a projector
\Pi_{i}=\sum_{n}\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right|
. The projectors are chosen to be orthogonal and to form a complete set, i.e.,
\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}=\delta_{ij}\Pi_{i}
and
\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}=\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. In an ideal measurement, upon obtaining the outcome “
i
”, one may further conclude that the post-measurement
system
is left in the state
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. This is one of three features that can be identified for ideal projective measurements. As explained in the main text, ideal measurements are

unbiased
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}]=\rho_{ii}\,\forall\,i,

(A.2)

faithful
:

\displaystyle C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\;\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]=1,

(A.3)

non-invasive
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\ \tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]=\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\bigr]=\rho_{ii}\,\forall\,i.

(A.4)

A.II
Example: 2-Qubit Measurements

To illustrate the properties above and to understand why these conditions are not met by general non-ideal measurement procedures with finite energy input, we consider a simple example. Consider a measurement procedure using a single pointer qubit and assume that by some means it has been prepared in the ground state, i.e.,
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. We model the interaction with the pointer by applying a controlled NOT operation
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}=\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}+\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes X_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, with the usual Pauli operator
X=\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|
. Denoting the matrix elements of the initial state as
\rho_{ij}=\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle
, we can then write the post-measurement state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}

\displaystyle=\,U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}\,\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}^{\dagger}\,=\,\sum_{i,j=0,1}\rho_{ij}\left|\right.\!{ii}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{jj}\!\left.\right|\,.

(A.5)

The system and pointer are now perfectly (classically) correlated in the sense that whenever the pointer is found in the state
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
(
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
), the system is left in the corresponding state
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
(
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
). In other words, for the choice
\Pi_{i}=\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, we find that the measurement is faithful,

\displaystyle C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})

\displaystyle=\,\sum\limits_{i=0,1}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{ii}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{ii}\!\left.\right|\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,\sum\limits_{i=0,1}\rho_{ii}\,=\,\mathrm{tr}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}]\,=\,1\,.

(A.6)

The post-measurement system state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}[\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]=\sum_{i}\rho_{ii}\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is in general different from the initial system state since it not longer has any off-diagonal elements w.r.t. the measurement basis, but the measurement is nonetheless non-invasive since the diagonal elements match those of the initial system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. At the same time, the chosen unitary
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
guarantees that the probabilities for finding the pointer in the states
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
and
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, match those of the original system state, i.e., for
i=0,1
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{tr}(\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})\,=\,\rho_{ii}\,.\vskip-5.69054pt

(A.7)

Consequently, the measurement is not biased towards one of the outcomes and reproduces the statistics of the original system state, while being perfectly correlated (i.e., faithful).

However, in general strong correlation and unbiasedness of the measurement do not imply one another. For instance, one can construct an unbiased but also generally uncorrelated measurement by replacing
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
with
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{SWA\hskip-0.7ptP}$}}}=\left|\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{01}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{10}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{10}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{01}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right|
, leaving the system in the state
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
no matter which state the pointer is in. Although all available information about the pre-measurement system is thus stored in the pointer, measuring the latter reveals no (additional) information about the post-measurement system. Alternatively, consider the unitary
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}+\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes X_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
instead of
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
, both of which lead to the same correlation
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
, but the probabilities for observing the two outcomes are now exchanged w.r.t. to
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, i.e., the pointer is found in the state
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
(
\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
) with probability
\rho_{11}
(
\rho_{00}
) after the interaction.

For the purpose of examining real measurements, these examples are of course pathological due to the assumption of reliably preparing the pointer in a pure state (without having to have performed a projective measurement in order to model a projective measurement or having to cool to the ground state using finite resources
[
12
]
). Let us therefore relax this assumption and assume instead that the pointer has been prepared at some finite non-vanishing temperature
T=1/\beta
, such that
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=p\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}+(1-p)\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
for some
p=(1+e^{-\omega\beta})^{-1}=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}
with
0<p<1
. A quick calculation then reveals that the previously perfect correlations are reduced to
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})=p=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}<1
and that the measurement procedure using
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
is in general biased, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}[\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{ii}(2p-1)+1-p\,.

(A.8)

However, while we generally have to give up the notion of a perfect projective measurement in the sense that the outcomes are perfectly correlated with the post-measurement states (as we have shown in the main text), one may retain the unbiasedness of the measurement. That is, if we replace
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
by
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{unb.}$}}}=\left|\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{01}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{10}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{10}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{01}\!\left.\right|
, we obtain the same imperfect correlation value
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})=p=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}
, but the unbiasedness condition of Eq. (
A.7
) is satisfied. To reiterate, measurement procedures using finite resources (finite time, finite energy, and finite complexity, i.e., operations with finite interaction range), cannot be ideal, since finite resources are not sufficient to prepare pointers in the required pure states. Realistic measurement procedures hence are non-ideal.

A.III
Non-Ideal Measurement Procedures

When any one of the three properties (
A.2
), (
A.3
) or (
A.4
) fails to hold, we call the corresponding measurement procedure
non-ideal
. For non-ideal measurements, the relation between the remaining properties is more complicated. In particular, none of the three properties alone implies any of the other two. For instance, consider an ideal post-interaction state

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{i}\rho_{ii}\,\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|\otimes\rho^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}+\,\text{off-diag.}\,,\vskip-5.69054pt

(A.9)

where we have not explicitly written the off-diagonal elements w.r.t. the basis
\{\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\otimes\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(j)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle\}_{i,j,n}
and
\rho^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
is a pointer state that is associated to one and only one of the outcomes
i
, that is,
\Pi_{i}\rho^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(j)$}}}=\delta_{ij}\rho^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(j)$}}}
. Any measurement procedure based on a map
\mathcal{E}
for which the values
\rho_{ii}
in (
A.9
) are replaced with arbitrary probabilities
p_{i}\neq\rho_{ii}
, results in a joint post-interaction state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
satisfying (
A.3
), but not (
A.2
) or (
A.4
), resulting in a non-ideal measurement that is faithful, but neither unbiased or non-invasive. Similarly, the state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
obtained from a trivial interaction
\mathcal{E}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
complies with (
A.4
), but not with (
A.3
) or (
A.2
). Finally, the map
\mathcal{E}
realizing a complete exchange of the initial system and pointer states (assuming, for the purpose of this example that
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
), results in an unbiased (
A.2
) measurement procedure that does not satisfy either (
A.3
) or (
A.4
).

Satisfying any single one of the three properties is hence not sufficient for distinguishing ideal from non-ideal measurements. When two out of the three properties hold, things become more subtle. In two cases, a joint final state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
satisfying a
pair
of properties implies the third property, and hence that the measurement is ideal for the particular given initial system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. First, a measurement that is both faithful (
A.3
)
and
unbiased (
A.2
) implies that it is also non-invasive (
A.4
). To show this, we start with the property of unbiasedness and, summing the right-hand side of Eq. (
A.2
) over all
i
, we have
\sum_{i}\rho_{ii}=1
. The left-hand side of Eq. (
A.2
) thus gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,1.

(A.10)

At the same time, property (
A.3
) demands that the sum in the second step of (
A.10
) yields
1
already just for the terms where
i=j
, implying

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\;\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,0.

(A.11)

Since all diagonal matrix elements of a density operator are non-negative, this further implies
\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\;\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]=0

\forall i\neq j
, which we can insert back into (
A.2
) to see that
\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,\rho_{ii}
. Inserting all of this into the left-hand side of Eq. (
A.4
) together with
\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,\rho_{ii},

(A.12)

which concludes the proof that unbiased and faithful measurements are also non-invasive.

Second, a measurement that is both faithful (
A.3
)
and
non-invasive (
A.4
) is also unbiased (
A.2
). Now starting with (
A.4
), we again sum the left-hand side over all
i
and resolve the identity
\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}
, to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{j}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{i}\rho_{ii}\,=\,1.

(A.13)

This time, unbiasedness (
A.2
) implies

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{j}\;\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\,0,

(A.14)

and in turn
\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{j}\;\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]=0

\forall i\neq j
, as before. Inserting this into (
A.4
) then implies
\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,\rho_{ii}
. Finally inserting into the left-hand side of (
A.2
) yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,=\,\rho_{ii},

(A.15)

confirming the unbiasedness condition.

For the remaining combination this is not the case. A measurement procedure that is unbiased (
A.2
) and non-invasive (
A.4
) for a fixed system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is not necessarily faithful (
A.3
). Consider, e.g., the initial single-qubit system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\tfrac{3}{4}\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right|+\tfrac{1}{4}\left|\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{1}\!\left.\right|
, i.e., where
\rho_{00}=\tfrac{3}{4}
and
\rho_{11}=\tfrac{1}{4}
, and the two-qubit final state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\sum_{m,n=0,1}\left|\right.\!{m,n}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{m,n}\!\left.\right|
with
p_{01}=p_{10}=p_{11}=\tfrac{1}{8}
and
p_{00}=\tfrac{5}{8}
. For
\Pi_{i}=\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, one has the reduced states
\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})=\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, so we have unbiasedness and non-invasiveness, but
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})=\tfrac{3}{4}<1
.

Moreover, the measurement procedure (corresponding to the transformation above (whose details are not given in the example) may not be unbiased or non-invasive for other initial system states
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. This singles out the property of faithfulness when one is interested in checking the properties of a measurement procedure for
any
given initial system state. Nonetheless, caution is advisable here. For a particular initial state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
of the system, all three properties may be satisfied, yet, this may not be so for other initial states. Simply consider the example in Eq. (
A.8
). For an initial state with
\rho_{00}=\rho_{11}=\tfrac{1}{2}
, the measurement satisfies all three properties, but for
p<1
the measurement procedure is biased and has non-perfect correlation for almost all (other) states
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
.

Indeed, demanding that any of the properties hold only for particular initial system states
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is somewhat contradictory to the notion of performing a measurement that reveals previously unknown information about a system. In other words, measurements should not require detailed knowledge about
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
to ensure that one may trust the measurement outcomes, or inferences made from them. The definitions of the properties (
A.2
), (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) must hence be extended to demand that measurement procedures are only called unbiased, faithful, or non-invasive, if the respective properties (
A.2
), (
A.3
) or (
A.4
) hold
for all initial systems states

\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
.

With such an extended definition, one then indeed finds that any two properties imply the third. In particular, it is then the case that measurement procedures that are unbiased
and
non-invasive, are also faithful, and thus ideal. The proof of this statement, relies on insights into the general structure of all maps representing unbiased measurement procedures, and as such appears later in Appendix
A.VI
. In purely qualitative terms, maps that are either unbiased or non-invasive need to transfer the diagonal elements of the system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
to particular (different) subspaces of the joint Hilbert space of system and pointer. The only way to simultaneously satisfy both the requirements for unbiasedness and non-invasiveness for arbitrary
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
forces all information to be concentrated in the subspaces corresponding to the images of the projectors
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|\otimes\Pi_{i}
, such that the resulting state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
satisfies (
A.3
) independently of the details of
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
.

Ultimately, this means that only one of the three properties can be satisfied exactly for all initial system states in any realistic measurement procedure. Given that the constraint of finite resources rules out that realistic non-ideal measurements are faithful, we have a choice between the measurement being unbiased or non-invasive. Arguably, biased measurements that are not even faithful are of little use, since the outcomes would not provide any level of certainty about either the pre- or post-measurement system state. In the following, we are therefore interested in unbiased measurement procedures for which the correlations between the system and the pointer are as large as possible. Given such non-ideal measurement, we then wish to minimize the associated energy costs.

A.IV
General Unbiased Measurements

Here, we will identify the basic structure and important properties of a general model of non-ideal measurement procedures. To do so, we separate what we believe to accurately model such a measurement procedure into two steps. These are:

I

Preparation
: Some energy is invested to prepare the pointer system in a suitable quantum state.

II

Correlating
: The pointer interacts with the system to be measured, creating correlations between them.

A.IV.1
Step I: Preparation

Before interacting with the system, the pointer can be prepared in a suitable quantum state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
at the expense of some initial energy investment
\Delta E_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}
accounting for the (CPTP) transformation
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})
mapping
\tau(\beta)
to
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}[\tau(\beta)]
. In particular it may be desirable to lower the entropy of the initial pointer state. In principle, one may use any given amount of energy to prepare an arbitrary pointer state that is compatible with the specified energy and whose entropy is lower than that of
\tau(\beta)
. The energy cost for reaching a particular state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
is bounded from below by the free energy difference, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Delta E_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}

\displaystyle\geq\,\Delta F\bigl(\tau(\beta)\rightarrow\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\bigr)\,=\,\Delta E_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}-T\Delta S_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\,,

(A.16)

with
\Delta E_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\mathrm{tr}\bigl[H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}(\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}-\tau(\beta))\bigr]
and
\Delta S_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=S\bigl(\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\bigr)-S\bigl(\tau(\beta)\bigr)
, and where
S(\rho)=-\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\rho\log(\rho)\bigr]
is the von Neumann entropy. However, the exact work cost of the preparation depends on the control over the system and the available auxiliary degrees of freedom, and may exceed this bound. In particular, the free energy difference to the ground state is finite although this state cannot be reached with a finite work investment in finite time
[
12
]
. The precise resource requirements in terms of energy, control, and time for preparing arbitrary quantum states are hence difficult to capture
3
3

3

Certainly, any such preparation can be described by a CPTP map, which in turn can be seen as a unitary acting on
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\Phi}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{\Phi}\!\left.\right|
in a Hilbert space enlarged by an auxiliary system with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$A$}}}\ni\left|\right.\!{\Phi}\!\left.\right\rangle
. However, this brings one back to the question of quantifying the cost for preparing the pure state
\left|\right.\!{\Phi}\!\left.\right\rangle
of the auxiliary system.
, whereas the refrigeration of quantum systems is a well-understood task, whose energy cost has been quantified for various levels of control one assumes about the quantum systems involved in the cooling procedure
[
16
]
.

It is therefore practically useful (and reasonable) to assume that the preparation only involves refrigeration. That is, we assume in the following that the temperature of the pointer is lowered from
T
to
T_{0}\leq T
, reaching a thermal state
\tau(\beta_{0})
with
\beta_{0}=1/T_{0}
. On the one hand, step
I
thus becomes less general than it could potentially be since one does not explore the entire Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. On the other hand, the thermal state can be considered to be energetically optimal, since it minimizes the energy at fixed entropy. Moreover, at fixed energy the thermal state also maximizes the entropy and hence minimizes the free energy, which in turn bounds the work cost from below.

A.IV.2
Step II: Correlating

During the second step of the measurement procedure, the system interacts with the pointer in such a way that correlations between the two are established via a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}:\,\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
that maps
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
to
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}\bigl[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]
.
A particularly important special case is the case of unitary correlating maps
U
, i.e., such that
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=U\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}U^{\dagger}
, representing measurement procedures where the joint system of
S
and
P
can be considered to be closed for the purpose of the correlating step. Then, the energy cost for the second step can be calculated via

\displaystyle\Delta E_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{tr}\bigl[(H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}+H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}-\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\bigr].

(A.17)

In any case the generated correlations can in principle be (but need not be) genuine quantum correlations. For (non-ideal) projective measurements as defined here, it nonetheless suffices that classical correlations are established with respect to the measurement basis (here the eigenbasis of
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
) and a chosen basis of the pointer system. More specifically, we assign a set of orthogonal projectors

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}

\displaystyle:=\,\sum\limits_{n}\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right|,

(A.18)

with
\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}=\delta_{ij}\Pi_{i}
(in particular,
\left\langle\right.\hskip-1.0pt\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}\hskip-1.0pt\left|\right.\hskip-1.0pt\tilde{\psi}_{n}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(j)$}}}\hskip-1.0pt\left.\right\rangle=\delta_{ij}\delta_{mn}
) and
\sum_{i}\Pi_{i}=\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. The orthogonality and completeness of the projectors ensure that every pointer state is associated with a state of the measured system, i.e., every outcome provides a definitive measurement result “i”. We further amend the correlations function defined in Eq. (
A.6
) for a single-qubit pointer to reflect the use of the more general projectors, i.e., we redefine the quantifier
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
as

\displaystyle C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})

\displaystyle:=\,\sum\limits_{i}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\Pi_{i}\,\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\bigr]\,.

(A.19)

A.IV.3
Unbiased measurements

We are now in a position to give a formal definition of what we consider as an abstract measurement procedure.

Definition 1:
Measurement procedure

A measurement procedure
\mathcal{M}(\beta)
that realizes a (non-ideal) projective measurement at ambient temperature
T=1/\beta
of an (unknown) quantum state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})
w.r.t. to an orthonormal basis
\{\left|\right.\!{n}\!\left.\right\rangle\}_{n}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is given by the tuple
(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}},H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}},\Pi,\mathcal{E})
, consisting of a pointer Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, a pointer Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})
, a complete set
\Pi=\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i}
of orthogonal projectors on
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, and a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
with
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, together with the induced CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{M}}:\ \rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\mapsto\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\mathcal{E}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\tau(\beta)].

(A.20)

Note that any definition of a thermal state
\tau(\beta)
implies that the state has full rank. This definition includes, in particular, the case that we consider here, where the map
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}\circ(\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}})
is split into two separate steps. As we have already motivated in our earlier example, we are interested in considering measurement procedures that represent the measured quantum state without bias. While perfect correlations cannot be guaranteed in this way, one may however ask that averages of the measured quantity match for the pointer and the system. Moreover, it is desirable that this is so independently of the specific initial states of the system and the pointer. All of this is captured by the following definition.

Definition 2:
Unbiased measurement

A measurement procedure
\mathcal{M}(\beta)
is called
unbiased
, iff

\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\Pi_{i}\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})\bigr]=\mathrm{tr}[\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}]

\forall i
and
\forall\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
.

Since we wish to restrict our further considerations to unbiased measurements, it will be useful to know more about the structure of these measurement procedures, in particular, about the involved CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
and projectors
\Pi_{i}
, given that one has selected a suitable pointer system with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
and Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
. To this end, note that our previous example using
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
was unbiased only for pointers that can be prepared in the ground state (or any pure state for that matter). This can only be the case if the initial temperature vanishes or if infinite resources are available in step
I
, whereas we are interested in describing more realistic conditions. To capture this, we formalise the following:

Definition 3:
Finite-resource measurement

A measurement procedure
\mathcal{M}(\beta)
at a nonzero ambient temperature
T=1/\beta
uses
finite resources
if the map
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{M}}
is rank non-increasing.

On the other hand, measurement procedures which
reduce
the rank use either infinite energy
E
, take infinite time
t
(an infinite sequence of finite interaction range operations) or are infinitely complex (infinite interaction range operations)
[
12
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
27
]
. Now, in order to take a first step towards unraveling the structure of unbiased measurements we formulate the following lemma.

Lemma 1

All unbiased finite-resource measurement procedures
\mathcal{M}(\beta)
with (thermal, full-rank) pointer system with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, Hamiltonian
H_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, and orthogonal projectors
\Pi_{i}
can be realized by CPTP maps
\mathcal{E}
of the form
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}\circ(\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}})
, where
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}
is a CPTP map from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})
to itself (achievable in finite time
t
and satisfying
\Delta E_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}<\infty
), and the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
to itself has Kraus operators
K_{l}=\sum\limits_{i}K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
with

\displaystyle K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}-1}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{d_{i}-1}k_{jmn}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i,l)$}}}\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{\psi_{n}}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}},

(A.21)

with
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}})
,
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}})\geq d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, and coefficients
k_{jmn}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i,l)$}}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}(K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}})^{\dagger}K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right|_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}.

(A.22)

Proof of Lemma

A.IV.3
. Before we get into the technical details of the proof, let us phrase the Lemma
A.IV.3
more informally. It states that the map
\mathcal{E}
consists of an arbitrary (finite energy,
\Delta E_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}<\infty
, finite time
t<\infty
) preparation of the pointer (
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}
), followed by a map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
that maps the subspaces
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
to those corresponding to
\Pi_{i}
, respectively. Moreover, note that unbiasedness of course implies that the pointer system must be large enough (
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\geq d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
) to accommodate all the possible measurement outcomes. Let us then prove the lemma. As mentioned before, the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
may be separated into a map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}
acting nontrivially only on the pointer Hilbert space, and a CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
acting on the resulting state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, which we can write with respect to the basis
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
as

\displaystyle\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.56064pt}{9.95863pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Without loss of generality, we can then write the final state

\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]
with respect to the product basis
\{\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(j)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\}_{i,j,m}
in the form

where we have indicated the columns corresponding to the subspaces of fixed vectors
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
and projectors
\Pi_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, and dots indicate matrix elements that may be nonzero but are not explicitly shown. In particular, the
latter can include subspaces for
i>3
, and the case for
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\leq 3
can be obtained by truncating the shown matrix by removing the corresponding rows and columns. The colored sub-blocks
A_{0i}
,
A_{1i}
,
A_{2i}
and so forth are
d_{i}\times d_{i}
block matrices in terms of which the unbiasedness condition of Def.
A.IV.3
can be written as

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\mathrm{tr}[A_{ji}]

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{ii}\ \ \forall i.

(A.31)

Crucially, the unbiasedness condition in Eq. (
A.31
) is to hold for all possible system states
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, and hence for all possible values of
\rho_{ii}
. This, in turn, implies that all sub-blocks with second subscript
i
must be proportional to
\rho_{ii}
. That is
A_{ji}=\rho_{ii}\tilde{A}_{ji}

\;\forall\,j
with

\sum_{j=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\mathrm{tr}[\tilde{A}_{ji}]=1

\forall i
. Since the unbiasedness condition is not sensitive to terms appearing in the off-diagonal blocks, a convenient representation of the relevant terms of the post-interaction state
\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
under the map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
is :

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}}=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}}{}

\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{array}\right]\,,

which we call the
correlation matrix
. Here it can immediately be seen that the unbiasedness condition, which implies
\sum_{j=0}^{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1}\mathrm{tr}[\tilde{A}_{ji}]=1
says that the sum of the trace of the blocks in column
i
of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{E}}
must be equal to
\rho_{ii}
for unbiasedness to hold
4
4

4

Note that this representation is not square, since in principle the dimension of the matrix-valued entries of each column are different. In the case that the map
\mathcal{E}
representing the measurement is unitary, the representation becomes square and the dimension of all blocks across all columns is equal.
.

From the initial state (
A.IV.3
) and the final state (
) it becomes apparent that unbiasedness can be guaranteed for maps that connect the subspaces corresponding to
\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
only with those corresponding to
\Pi_{i}
. More precisely, each of these connecting maps can be viewed as an arbitrary CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
from the
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
-dimensional space spanned by the vectors in the set
\{\left|\right.\!{i}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\psi_{n}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\}_{n=0,\ldots,d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}-1}
, where
\{\left|\right.\!{\psi_{n}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\}_{n}
is an arbitrary basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
, to the
(d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\times d_{i})
-dimensional space spanned by the vectors in the set
\{\left|\right.\!{j}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\begin{subarray}{c}j=0,\ldots,d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1\\
m=0,\ldots,d_{i}-1\end{subarray}$}}}
. The Kraus operators for these CPTP maps are precisely the
\{K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}\}_{l}
of Eq. (
A.21
) and in matrix notation we may denote these maps as

\displaystyle\rho_{ii}\rho_{P}

\displaystyle\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}\\
\longmapsto\end{subarray}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\!\!A_{0i}&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\cdot&\!\!A_{1i}&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdot&\!\!A_{2i}&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\vdots&\!\!\vdots&\!\!\vdots&\!\!\ddots\end{array}\right)=\,\rho_{ii}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\!\!\tilde{A}_{0i}&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\cdot&\!\!\tilde{A}_{1i}&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\cdot&\!\!\cdot&\!\!\tilde{A}_{2i}&\!\!\cdots\\
\hline\cr\!\!\vdots&\!\!\vdots&\!\!\vdots&\!\!\ddots\end{array}\right).

Since the domains as well as the images for different
i
lie in orthogonal subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
, the maps
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
can be combined to the map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
with Kraus operators
5
5

5

Note that the number of nonzero Kraus operators may be different for each
i
, but one may always add trivial (vanishing) Kraus operators to each set
\{K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}\}_{l}
with fixed
i
.

\{K_{l}=\sum_{i}K_{l}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}\}_{l}
. Once can check that the unbiasedness condition is satisfied for these Kraus operators by a simple calculation, which we will not repeat here. If the initial ambient temperature is nonzero and the measurement procedure uses finite resources (time and energy), the pointer state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
has full rank and unbiasedness can only be achieved via maps of the form mentioned, as claimed in Lemma
A.IV.3
, which concludes the proof. ∎

Inspecting again the example from Appendix
A.II
, one notes that the controlled NOT operation
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
is not of the form required for a finite-resource unbiased measurement, as expected. However, when the pointer can be prepared in a pure state (w.l.o.g. the ground state
\left|\right.\!{0}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
) one observes that the measurement procedure using
U_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{CNOT}$}}}
in the correlating step becomes unbiased because some of the sub-blocks
A_{ji}
are only trivially proportional to
\rho_{ii}
. In particular,
A_{01}=\tilde{A}_{01}=A_{10}=\tilde{A}_{10}=0
.

Having understood the general structure of all unbiased measurements, we now want to turn to some specific instances of such measurement procedures.

A.V
Extremal Measurements

With the help of Lemma
A.IV.3
we can now describe the set of
all
unbiased measurements for a given quantum system
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
and pointer. The measurement within this set may further be categorized according to their specific properties, in particular, their energy cost, the amount of correlations created between the system and the pointer (how faithful the measurement is), and the level of control required for their implementation (e.g., what type of auxiliary systems are available and which operations can be performed on the system, pointer, and auxiliaries). Given an (unknown) quantum system
S
it would ideally be desirable to answer the question: What is the maximal correlation achievable between the system and
any suitable
pointer given a fixed work input
\Delta E
? A more restricted version of this question is: What is the maximal correlation achievable between the system and
a particular
pointer given a fixed work input
\Delta E
?

Since we assume that the system state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is unknown before the measurement, the correlation measure
\bar{C}
that we are interested in optimizing is obtained from averaging
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
from Eq. (
A.3
) over all system states. We observe that for any particular systems state
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, the correlation measure
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
does not depend on any of the matrix elements of
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
except for those on the diagonal, i.e.,

\displaystyle C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})

\displaystyle=\,\rho_{00}\mathrm{tr}[\tilde{A}_{00}]+\rho_{11}\mathrm{tr}[\tilde{A}_{11}]+\rho_{22}\mathrm{tr}[\tilde{A}_{22}]+\dots\,.

(A.44)

Averaging over all states
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
is hence equivalent to an average over all probability distributions corresponding to the diagonal of
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
. Moreover, for each of these values
\rho_{ii}

(i=0,\ldots,d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}-1)
, the average over all probability distributions results in the value
1/d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, such that the average of
C(\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}})
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{C}

\displaystyle=\,\tfrac{1}{d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}}\mathrm{tr}\bigl[\tilde{A}_{00}+\tilde{A}_{11}+\tilde{A}_{22}+\dots\bigr],

(A.45)

which, in terms of the representation presented in Eq. (
A.IV.3
), corresponds to taking the trace of the blocks appearing along the diagonal.

Despite this simple form of
\bar{C}
, the optimization over all pointer systems and operations thereon is a daunting task. Indeed, even for a fixed pointer at initial temperature
T=1/\beta
, identifying the optimal measurement procedure in terms of the best ratio of (average) correlation increase per unit energy cost (averaged over the input system states) is highly nontrivial. To illustrate the difficulty, first note that an (attainable) bound exists for correlating (quantified by the mutual information) two arbitrary systems that are initially thermal at the same temperature at optimal energy expenditure
[
36
,
37
]
. While the known protocol for attaining this bound is in general not unitary (it involves lowering the temperature), in some cases the bound is tight already when one correlates the systems unitarily. However, it was recently shown
[
38
,
39
]
that the optimal (in the sense of the mentioned bound being tight) trade-off between correlations and energy cost cannot always be achieved unitarily.

Of course, in our case, the initial state of the system is not known, and cannot be expected to be thermal in general. Moreover, the mutual information is not a suitable figure of merit for quantifying the desired correlations between system and pointer because the latter don’t distinguish between classical and genuinely quantum correlations. For instance, for a single-qubit system and pointer, the states
\left|\right.\!{\Phi^{+}}\!\left.\right\rangle_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\bigl(\left|\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right\rangle+\left|\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right\rangle\bigr)/\sqrt{2}
and
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(\left|\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{00}\!\left.\right|+\left|\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{11}\!\left.\right|\bigr)
have different values of mutual information but are equally well (i.e., perfectly) correlated w.r.t. to the desired measurement basis. The above arguments on optimally correlating protocols hence do not apply directly, but with the added complication of the unknown system state and the unbiasedness condition, we cannot rule out the possibility that the optimal unbiased measurement procedures are not realized by a unitary correlating step.

Nonetheless, it can be argued that any nonunitary realization of the second part
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
of the CPTP map
\mathcal{E}
must require higher levels of control than a corresponding unitary realization due to the requirement of realizing nonunitary maps
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
as unitaries on a larger Hilbert space. Specifically, any CPTP map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
can be thought of as a unitary on a larger Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$E$}}}
(with a factoring initial condition)
[
40
]
, that is, one may write any
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{tr}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$E$}}}\bigl[U_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptPE$}}}\bigl[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\otimes\left|\right.\!{\chi}\!\left.\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle\right.\!{\chi}\!\left.\right|\bigr]U_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptPE$}}}^{\dagger}\bigr]

(A.46)

for some unitary
U_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptPE$}}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$E$}}}
and for some pure state
\left|\right.\!{\chi}\!\left.\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$E$}}}
. At the same time, employing a unitary to correlate pointer and system enables us to unambiguously quantify the work cost of the correlating step without assumptions about the Hamiltonian of potential auxiliary systems.

We are therefore particularly interested in describing all unbiased measurement procedures, where
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
is realized unitarily, such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}

\displaystyle=\,\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}[\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}]\,=\,U\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}U^{\dagger}

(A.47)

with
UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=\mathds{1}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S\hskip-1.0ptP$}}}
. In this sense, our focus lies on unbiased measurement procedures where all control that one may have over external systems (beyond
S
and
P
) is used in the initial step represented by
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I}$}}}
to prepare the pointer in a suitable state, e.g., by lowering its temperature. Here we make use of the fact that the work cost of refrigeration with various levels of control has been extensively studied
[
16
]
. This leaves us with the task of analyzing the structure of the representations
U
of the unitary maps
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
.

A first step towards the completion of this task is to note that the unbiasedness condition for measurement procedures with a
unitary
correlating step
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
means that it is inefficient (in terms of energy cost) to use a pointer Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
whose dimension is not an integer multiple of the system dimension. This can be explained in the following way. By inspection of the maps in Eq. (
A.IV.3
), one notes that
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
maps the
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}\times d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
density matrix
\rho_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}
to a
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\operatorname{rank}(\Pi_{i})\times d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\operatorname{rank}(\Pi_{i})
density matrix. That is, the size of each of the
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
blocks
\tilde{A}_{ji}

\;\forall\,j
is determined by the rank of
\Pi_{i}
. If the map
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}
is unitary, this implies that all
\mathcal{E}_{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$\mathrm{I\hskip-0.5ptI}$}}}^{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$(i)$}}}
are unitary, and hence

\displaystyle d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}

\displaystyle=\,d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}\,\operatorname{rank}(\Pi_{i})\quad\forall\,i.

(A.48)

Conversely, this implies that all projectors
\Pi_{i}
have the same rank
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}/d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
, which must be an integer larger or equal to 1,
d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$P$}}}=\lambda d_{\hskip-0.5pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\tiny{$S$}}}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. The implication of this for the correlation matrix in (
A.IV.3
) is that it is now square

\displaystyle\Gamma_{U_{\text{unb}}}=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}}{}
